A Usage-Aware Sequent Calculus for Differential Dynamic Logic CCS: Theory of computation Logic and verification CCS: Theory of computation Program analysis CCS: Software and its engineering Error handling and recovery
Myra Dotzel, Stefan Mitsch, André Platzer

Abstract
. Ensuring that safety-critical applications behave as intended is an important yet challenging task. Modeling languages like differential dynamic logic ( dL ) have proof calculi capable of proving guarantees for such applications. However, dL programmers may unintentionally over-specify assumptions and program statements, which results in overly constrained models that yield weak or vacuous guarantees. In hybrid systems models, such constraints are ubiquitous; they may appear as assumptions, conditions on control switches, and evolution domain constraints in systems of differential equations which makes it nontrivial to systematically detect which ones are over-specified. Existing approaches are limited, either lacking formal correctness guarantees or the granularity to detect all kinds of bugs arising in a given formula. As a remedy, we present a novel proof-based technique that detects which constraints in a dL formula are vacuous or over-specified and suggests ways in which these components could be mutated while preserving correctness proofs. When properties follow entirely from constraints uninfluenced by program statements, this analysis spots outright flaws in models. Otherwise, it helps make models more flexible by identifying specific ways in which they may be generalized. The resulting analysis is thorough, catching bugs at a fine-grained level and proposing mutations that could be applied in combination. We prove soundness and completeness with respect to dL to ensure the correctness of suggested mutations and general applicability of our technique.



1.
Introduction

Hybrid systems verification establishes correctness properties of safety-critical cyber-physical systems models like collision avoidance for aircraft
(
Squires et al. 2018
)
, autonomous vehicles
(
Pek and Althoff 2020
)
, and trains
(
Platzer and Quesel 2009
)
.
Programmers who verify such properties first write a model that describes the physical behavior of their system. Then, they attempt to show that the desired properties hold after running the program under a certain set of assumptions. To build a proof, a programmer may apply a series of rules to dissect the model into a series of smaller proof obligations until elementary facts are reached.

However, the results proven are only useful if the models adequately capture the physical dynamics of the system
(
Mitsch and Platzer 2016
;
Selvaraj et al. 2022
)
.
As most cyber-physical systems models aspire to replicate realistic behavior, they are inherently difficult to write and analyze, combining descriptions of control algorithms with descriptions of the physical dynamics of a given application
(
Alur 2015
;
Lee and Seshia 2015
;
Platzer 2010
;
Platzer 2018
)
. Modeling languages like differential dynamic logic (
dL
)
(
Platzer 2008
;
Platzer 2017
)
represent control algorithms by a discrete program and physical dynamics by a system of differential equations and evolution domain constraints on which the dynamics apply.
While uncertainty about the fidelity of the control model can be overcome by verified compilation
(
Bohrer et al. 2018
)
, the physics model requires a combination of offline and online verification at runtime
(
Mitsch and Platzer 2016
)
.

Due to the difficulty of writing hybrid systems models,
dL
formulas are prone to two classes of modeling errors: vacuity and over-specification
(
Selvaraj et al. 2022
)
.
The first can be overcome by vacuity detection which determines vacuous components in temporal logic formulas
(
Kupferman and Vardi 2003
)
for cyber-physical systems
(
Dokhanchi et al. 2017
)
.
To address the second, prior work has used techniques like mutation testing to determine over-specified components of cyber-physical systems models and suggest alternatives
(
Bartocci et al. 2023
)
.
However, these techniques are limited to finding a single bug, whereas in practice there may be many, and are infeasible to implement naively. In particular, proofs involving real arithmetic, which are common in
dL
, are doubly-exponential in the number of variables
(
Davenport and Heintz 1988
)
which makes the combinatorial exploration of proof alternatives inherent to mutation testing expensive
(
Grebing and Ulbrich 2020
)
.
To our knowledge, there are also no formal guarantees that ensure the correctness and general applicability of these tools.

Recent work applies weakest precondition and strongest postcondition techniques to find bugs in
dL
formulas
(
Chong et al. 2023
)
.
However, bugs may occur anywhere in a
dL
formula, not just in the pre- and post-condition. In
dL
formulas,
constraints
about parameters, initial states, conditionals in control algorithms, and evolution domain constraints – which occur spread throughout a formula – all regulate the conditions under which a safety property applies. For example, a safety proof about the effects of a hybrid systems controller is vacuous if safety already follows directly from evolution domain constraints which are actually constraints on the physics and not conclusions about the safety of the control actions.
These challenges are not unique to
dL
formulas; they plague hybrid automata too
(
Alur et al. 1993
;
Henzinger 2000
)
.
For this reason, detecting over-specification in
dL
formulas is a challenging yet important task for strengthening safety guarantees of the hybrid systems these formulas are intended to model.

Our key observation in this work is that carefully inspecting hybrid systems correctness proofs provides insight on the fidelity
(
Platzer 2018
)
(and over-specification) of their models.

We develop
UAPC
a sequent calculus for
dL
that tracks constraint usage in a proof. Augmenting the
dL
sequent calculus with a dataflow analysis,
UAPC
computes proof-preserving
mutations
of constraints in each proof step.
The tracking of constraints and mutations serve complementary roles. Constraint tracking is useful while conducting the proof to determine where facts come from, and how they are linked to the original conjecture. Alternatively, mutations are most meaningful after the proof is finished where in a separate model improvement step a programmer selects from the tracked mutation alternatives.
We prove that
UAPC
is both sound and complete, giving a programmer assurance that applying the computed mutations to their formula would preserve its validity, and that
UAPC
could analyze
any
valid
dL
formula.

The techniques we introduce here are general enough that they could be extended to other proof systems, yet the significance and subtleties are special for hybrid systems.
To demonstrate the utility of these techniques, we developed
UAPC
for the
dL
proof calculus which handles safety-critical applications.

This paper makes the following contributions:

•

A novel core proof calculus
UAPC
that systematically tracks constraint usage in a proof.

•

UAPC
+
, the sequential counterpart of
UAPC
that reuses information from previously-analyzed proof branches to guide the analysis.

•

\texttt{UAPC}_{\texttt{diag}}
, and extension of
UAPC
that diagnoses the usage of cuts in a proof.

•

Soundness and completeness of all calculi with respect to
dL
, verifying the analyses to be correct and applicable to any valid
dL
formula.

Outline.

In Sec.
2
, we recall differential dynamic logic (
dL
)
(
Platzer 2008
)
. Through an example, we study how the
dL
proof rules operate and speculate which constraints in the original conjecture are over-specified by eyeballing the proof in an ad-hoc fashion. In Sec.
3
and
4
, we develop the
UAPC
proof calculus which is capable of detecting over-specified constraints
automatically
. In Sec.
5
we provide the soundness and completeness properties of
UAPC
and sketch their proofs. In Sec.
6
, we revisit our original example, this time analyzing with
UAPC
. We conclude with discussions of related (Sec.
7
) and future work (Sec.
8
).

2.
Background and Motivation

In this section, we review
dL
. We first give its syntax (Sec.
2.1
). Then we show an example of a
dL
formula (Sec.
2.2
) and an example safety proof of the formula (Sec.
2.3
).

2.1.
Differential Dynamic Logic (
dL
)

dL
is a logic for specifying hybrid programs
(
Platzer 2008
)
. Programmers write
dL
formulas
to express properties of hybrid systems.
dL
programs
consist of statements and formulas, as shown below.

\displaystyle\mathit{Term}\quad e,d

\displaystyle::=x\mid c\mid e+d\mid e\cdot d\mid e/d\mid\sqrt{e}\mid f(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})

\displaystyle\mathit{Stmt}\quad\alpha,\beta

\displaystyle::=x:=e\mid x:=*\mid x^{\prime}=f(x),\dots,y^{\prime}=f(y)\&Q\mid\,?Q\mid\alpha;\beta\mid\alpha\cup\beta\mid\alpha^{*}

\displaystyle\mathit{Fml}\quad P,Q

\displaystyle::=\text{true}\mid\text{false}\mid e\sim d\mid p(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})\mid\lnot P\mid P\bowtie Q\mid\forall x\;P\mid\exists x\;P

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\mid[\alpha]P\mid\langle\alpha\rangle P

Terms
e,d
in
dL
are variables
x
, rational constants
c\in\mathbb{Q}
, polynomials, and functions of variable arguments.
dL
statements
\alpha,\beta
include deterministic and nondeterministic assignments of a term to a variable, differential equations (
x^{\prime}=f(x),\dots,y^{\prime}=f(y)
with evolution domain contraint
Q
), test statements (?), program sequencing (;), nondeterminstic choice (
\cup
), and repetition (*).
dL
formulas
P,Q
include boolean primitives, binary relations
\sim\;\in\{=,\geq,>\}
, boolean predicates, negation, boolean connectives
\bowtie\;\in\{\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\leftrightarrow\}
, universal and existential quantification, and two kinds of modalities
\left[\cdot\right]
(pronounced “box") and
\langle\cdot\rangle
(pronounced “diamond"). The modality
\left[\alpha\right]P
means that
P
holds after
all
runs of
\alpha
, whereas
\langle\alpha\rangle P
means that
P
holds after
some
run of hybrid program
\alpha
.

To prove the correctness of a
dL
formula, we reason about
sequents
which take the form
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
where
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite sets of
dL
formulas and the assumptions in
\Gamma
are used to prove
\Delta
. A sequent is
valid
if
\bigwedge_{p\in\Gamma}p\rightarrow\bigvee_{q\in\Delta}q
is true in all states.

2.2.
Parachute Model

For example, a programmer could write a
dL
formula that models the aerodynamics of a skydiver’s parachute (Fig.
1
). Constraints on the formula occur spread throughout the formula, which we
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{highlight}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for emphasis.

\displaystyle\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\to}\left[\left(\left(?(\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{r=r_{cl}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{x>100}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\cup\;r:=r_{op}\right);t:=0;\text{ODE}\&\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{Q}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\right)^{*}\right]P

\displaystyle\text{Parameters}~g,r_{op},v_{max},T,x,v,r,t

\displaystyle\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\triangleq\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{g>0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{r_{cl}=0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{r_{op}>r_{cl}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{T>0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{v_{max}<-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{x\geq 0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}<v<0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{r=r_{cl}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle P\triangleq\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{x=0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\to v\geq v_{max}

\displaystyle\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{Q}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\triangleq\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{t\leq T}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{x\geq 0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{v<0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\displaystyle\text{ODE}\triangleq x^{\prime}=v,v^{\prime}=-g+rv^{2},t^{\prime}=1

Figure 1.
Model of parachute dynamics, adopted from
(
Fulton et al. 2017
)
.

The program runs under assumptions
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{A}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. The overall program structure is a loop where
[(\cdot)^{*}]P
expresses that
P
holds after all iterations of the loop.

At the beginning of each loop iteration, the program may choose to run either the branch
?(\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{r=r_{cl}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\land\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{x>100}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
or the branch
r:=r_{op}
. The former allows the skydiver to keep falling when safe, if the aerodynamic drag coefficient is
r=r_{cl}
and the skydiver’s position is a safe distance from the ground (
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{x>100}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
).
The latter represents the parachute opening which induces an aerodynamic drag coefficient by setting
r
to the drag coefficient of the open parachute
r_{op}
.
After this choice, time
t
is reset to
0
and the program runs the dynamics as allowed by the domain constraint
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{Q}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. ODE models the dynamics of the skydiver’s parachute in free fall but where velocity is affected by aerodynamic drag, while
t^{\prime}=1
acts as a clock and
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{t\leq T}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
imposes a time limit
T
.
Domain constraint
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{Q}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
enforces that the skydiver’s position is above ground and that velocity is negative during evolution (parachute descends).

The postcondition
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{x=0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\to v\geq v_{max}
expresses that when the skydiver hits the ground (
\mathbf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{x=0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
), their velocity
v
will be bounded by an acceptable touchdown velocity
v_{max}
(
v\geq v_{max}
due to the non-positive velocity where
v_{max}
is considered “maximal" w.r.t. magnitude).

(3h)

\frac{\frac{(1)\hskip 8.19447pt(2)\hskip 8.19447pt(3)\hskip 8.19447pt\color[rgb]{1,0,0}u_{1}\text{ fresh}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{i}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\left[\left(\{?(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{j,k,l}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\displaystyle L(x,v)$});\cup\;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{m}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r:=r_{op};\};\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{n}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t:=0;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{o}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{ODE}\&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{p}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\right)^{*}\right](\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{q}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P)}\quad\textsc{rightstyle=,Right=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}${\text{loop}}$,width=}}{\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\rightarrow\left[\left(\{?(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{j,k,l}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\displaystyle L(x,v)$});\cup\;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{m}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r:=r_{op};\};\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{n}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t:=0;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{o}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{ODE}\&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{p}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\right)^{*}\right](\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{q}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P)}\quad\textsc{rightstyle=,Right=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}${{\to}R}$,width=}

where:

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{j,k,l}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L(x,v)\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\displaystyle v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}$}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{k}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=r_{cl}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{l}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$\displaystyle x>100$}

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8},u_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I(x,v)\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{5}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{6}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v<0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{7}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{8}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r_{\textit{cl}}=0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{u_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x>-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{o}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\qquad\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\dots{\mid}\Psi)=\{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{8}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{5}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{6}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{7}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{u_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{o}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Theta=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{5}{\curlywedgeuparrow}p_{2}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{6}{\curlywedgeuparrow}p_{3}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{p_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{7}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{8}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{2}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{3}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{u_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{5}{\curlywedgeuparrow}p_{2}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{6}{\curlywedgeuparrow}p_{3}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{2}\}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{3}\}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{7}\}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}m}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{p_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},

Figure 2.
Parachute example proof fragment in
UAPC
(with
red
,
blue
, and
violet
labels) or
dL
(without). We write
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\dots{\mid}\Psi)
for
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8},u_{1},j,k,l,n,r,s\,{\mid}\Psi)
.

2.3.
Parachute Proof

The parachute formula in Fig.
1
is valid
(
Fulton et al. 2017
)
, meaning that it has a proof in the
dL
proof calculus
(
Platzer 2012
)
.
We show a small chunk of an example
proof tree
in boxes in Fig.
2
(full syntax-directed proof in Appendix
A
). A zig-zag border at the top indicates that the proof continues in another snippet at the annotated subgoals, while a zig-zag border at the bottom indicates that the snippet continues from an open subgoal of another snippet. For now, we can ignore all the
red
,
blue
, and
violet
annotations and just focus on the
dL
proof tree (written in black) and the
yellow
highlights.

The proof begins in the bottom fragment of Fig.
2
, and should be read from conclusion (formula to prove valid) to premises (elementary facts that can be proven relatively easily).
The first proof step,
M:
\rightarrow
R, moves the left-hand side of the implication
A
into the set of assumptions on left of the turnstile. In the next step,
M:
loop introduces a
loop invariant

I(x,v)
, which is an intermediate formula to help complete the proof. The application of the
M:
loop rule spawns three proof branches, each corresponding to a smaller obligation to prove about the loop invariant: (1) that
I(x,v)
holds initially, (2) that
I(x,v)
can be used to prove the postcondition, and (3) that
I(x,v)
holds after one loop iteration.

We show the proof of obligation (3) in the middle fragment of Fig.
2
(branch (3a)). Here, the proof continues with
M:
[?]
which converts the test
[?L(x,v)][t:=0;\text{ODE}\&Q]I(x,v)
into an implication
L(x,v)\rightarrow[t:=0;\text{ODE}\&Q]I(x,v)
. After a few more steps, the proof reaches the step
M:
[]\land
(derivable from the axiom
M:
K) which breaks the formula into two separate conjuncts that should hold after running the same program.
To prove these separately, the rule
M:
\land
R, forks the proof into two branches, each one proving different properties of ODE.
The proof of the right conjunct follows by an application of the ule
M:
dC which uses a
differential cut

v\geq v_{0}-gt
to instead prove two smaller obligations: that the cut holds after program execution (branch shown) and that the cut can be used to prove the postcondition when inserted into the ODE domain constraint (branch (3f), see Appendix).
The step
M:
dI proves the former obligation by showing that the domain constraint implies the postcondition (branch (3g), see Appendix) and turning differential equations into assignments.

Branch (3h) uses these assignments to avoid needing to solve the differential equations they represent
(
Platzer and Tan 2018
;
Platzer 2017
)
. Here, each
M:
[:=]_{1}
step substitutes
t^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
with their respective terms in the assignments into the postcondition. Lastly, the leaf rule
M:
QE for real arithmetic is applied, concluding this proof branch.

By carefully inspecting the proof tree in Fig.
2
, we notice that the original model is
over-specified
.

In particular,
highlighted
components
x>100
and
v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}
are not
used
in the proof; they are neither necessary to prove the closing step
M:
QE in (3h) nor are they involved in proving the rest of the branch. Therefore, they could be removed entirely from the original conjecture while producing the same (or a similar) proof. Thus, a formula without this information, or some generalized form of it, would still uphold its original correctness properties.

In the next section, we will cover the key ideas of
UAPC
, a proof calculus that will help us catch these bugs automatically. We will explain the annotations in the Fig.
2
proof tree, which is the translation of a
dL
proof into
UAPC
, and formalize the removal and generalization of constraints as
mutations
applied to
dL
formulas at a fine-grained level.

3.
Key Ideas of Usage-Aware Sequents

Towards translating
dL
proofs into
UAPC
, we embellish
dL
sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
with annotations to obtain a
usage-aware sequent

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
where the annotations
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}},\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi,\Sigma
facilitate the data-flow analysis of a proof.

In this section, we define the key constructs of
UAPC
– atoms and their identifying labels (Sec.
3.1
), mutations (Sec.
3.2
), and sets that track labels and possible mutations (Sec.
3.3
). In Sec.
3.4
, we introduce a mutation operator that simulates applying mutations post-analysis.

3.1.
Atoms and Labels

The fine-grained model components that
UAPC
analyzes are called
atoms
. They are statements and formulas that cannot be further decomposed (defined below):

\displaystyle\textit{Atomic Stmt}\quad\alpha_{\text{atom}}

\displaystyle::=x:=e\mid x:=*\mid x^{\prime}=f(x)

\displaystyle\textit{Atomic Fml}\quad P_{\text{atom}}

\displaystyle::=\text{true}\mid\text{false}\mid e\sim d\mid p(e_{1}\dots e_{k})

Atoms include boolean primitives, binary operations, predicates, assignments, and differential equations (without their domain constraints, which are composed of more atoms).
In Fig.
1
,
x>100
,
v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}
, and
r:=r_{op}
are atoms whereas
ODE
is not due to the multiple equations.

To identify atoms and help track their usage throughout a proof, we introduce
labels

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l_{1},l_{2}}
. Labels are defined inductively below where
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l
denotes a
singleton label
, and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}_{1}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{l}_{2}
denotes a
fused label
.
In the conclusion of a proof, labels are all singletons, while fused labels are formed during the analysis to track relationships between atoms, effectively cross-labeling them. The relationship enforced by fused labels will be discussed in Sec.
3.3
.
In general, we write
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{1}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}i_{n}
to denote a fused label with
n
singleton labels (which could accumulate in a proof).
Fusing is commutative, meaning that
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{1}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}i_{n}
is equivalent to any fused permutation of
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{1},\dots,i_{n}
, and fused labels with duplicate singletons degenerate, e.g.,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l{\curlywedgeuparrow}l
is equivalent to writing
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l
. We use a
label vector

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{l}}
to denote a nonempty collection of labels.
We define labels and label vectors below:

\displaystyle\textit{Label}\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}::=

\displaystyle\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}_{1}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{l}_{2}

\displaystyle\textit{Label Vector}\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{l}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}::=

\displaystyle\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l},\vec{\mathbf{l}}

We assign each atom in a statement or formula an identifying label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathbf{l}}
, inductively defined below. We use label vectors
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{l}}
to denote the collection of labels in a compound statement
\alpha,\beta
or formula
P,Q
.

\displaystyle\textit{Labeled Stmt}\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha,{\beta}::=\,

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\mathbf{l}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x:=e{\kern-1.99997pt}\mid\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\mathbf{l}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x:=*{\kern-1.99997pt}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\mathbf{l}_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}=f(x),\dots,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\mathbf{l}_{n}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y^{\prime}=f(y)\&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}{\kern-1.99997pt}\mid\,?\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q{\kern-1.99997pt}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta}{\kern-1.99997pt}

\displaystyle\mid(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha)^{*}{\kern-1.99997pt}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\cup\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta}

\displaystyle\textit{Labeled Fml}\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,Q::=\,

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\mathbf{l}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{true}\mid\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\mathbf{l}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{false}\mid\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\mathbf{l}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e\sim d\mid\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\mathbf{l}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})\mid\neg\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\bowtie\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\mid\forall x\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P

\displaystyle\mid\exists x\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\mid{[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mid{\langle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\rangle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}

In the Fig.
2
definition of
I(x,v)
, the singleton labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{5}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{6}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{7}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}u_{1}
identify the atoms
x\geq 0
,
v<0
,
v>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}
,
x>-1
, respectively.

Properties.

Each atom in the usage-aware sequent
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
has a label in
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}
that the analysis uses to track its propagation throughout a proof.
Property
1
enforces that each atom in a
dL
formula is assigned a
singleton
and
unique
label before the analysis.
Labels in the conclusion of a proof are distinct from each other even if the same atom occurs multiple times in different places. The propagation of labels throughout a proof tree allows us to track atom usage, and may subsequently spawn
fresh
labels if new atoms are introduced (to be discussed in Sec.
4
).

Definition 3.1 (
Uniqueness
).

Let
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{i}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{j}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
. A singleton label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is
unique
iff
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\neq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\forall\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l
.

Definition 3.2 (
Freshness
).

A singleton label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l
is
fresh
for a valid sequent
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{i}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{j}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
iff
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\notin\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}
.

Property 1 (
Labeling schema
).

The labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}
in the conclusion of a proof
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{i}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{j}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
are singleton and unique. The labels of a proof tree contain either singletons that appear in the conclusion of the proof or that are fresh.

3.2.
Mutations

The data-flow analysis uses labels to determine which atoms in a formula could be
mutated
while preserving its proof.
In this work, we consider three different
mutation levels
—
id
,
G
, and
R
(pronounced identity, generalize, and remove).

These mutation levels (
mutations
, for short) are functions that take atomic statements to atomic statements, and atomic formulas to atomic formulas. They indicate
the extent
to which an atom can be modified, and
the goal of the analysis is to solve for these mutations for every atom in a
dL
formula.

We define the mutation
\mathcal{M}
in Fig.
3
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle:\textit{Atomic Stmt}\to\textit{Atomic Stmt},\textit{Atomic Fml}\to\textit{Atomic Fml}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbf{b}\qquad\qquad{\mathbf{b}\in\{\text{true},\text{false}\},\mathcal{M}\in\{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(e\sim d)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}e\sim d\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}={\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\\
e\geq d^{*}\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}={\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\\
\text{true}\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}={\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(p(e_{1}\dots e_{k}))

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}p(e_{1}\dots e_{k})\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}\in\{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}}\\
\text{true}\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}={\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(x:=e)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}x:=e\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}={\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\\
x:=d^{*}\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}={\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\\
?\text{true}\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}={\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(x:=*)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}x:=*\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}\in\{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}}\\
?\text{true}\;&\quad{\text{if}\;\mathcal{M}={\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\end{cases}

Figure 3.
Definition of mutation
\mathcal{M}

When applied,
id
produces an atom identical to its input whereas
R
removes an atom from a
dL
formula while maintaining its validity. The mutation
R
replaces formulas with “
true
” (uninformative constraint) and programs with “?
true
” (program without effect). Mutation
G
generalizes
e\sim d
(
e=d
,
e>d
, or
e\geq d
) to
e\geq d^{*}
for a term
d^{*}
such that the replacement of
e\sim d
with
e\geq d^{*}
in a
dL
formula preserves its proof. The term
d^{*}
is a fixed user-defined value with the same type as
d
. Because equalities (such as
x=e
) may come from assignments in a proof (to be discussed in Sec.
4
), we let the
G
mutation of
x:=e
be
x:=d^{*}
. Any mutation of the booleans true and false produces the same output because these are primitive.

We wait to define
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}
for differential equations because we treat their mutation together with their domain constraints which, as a whole, is not atomic. We instead consider them in Sec.
3.4
when discussing how to apply mutations to non-atomic formulas and statements.

Properties.

We observe that if an atom could be removed without affecting the validity of the original conjecture, then the atom could be generalized or remain identical, because removal implies that the atom is not used anywhere in the proof.
If an atom can be generalized, then it can remain identical too but should not be removed. If an atom must remain identical, then it should not be generalized or removed.
These observations characterize a semilattice over the mutations
\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and
R
(Defn.
3.3
).

Definition 3.3 (Mutation structure).

(\{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\},\sqcap)
forms a meet-semilattice where
\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sqsubseteq\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sqsubseteq\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

The mutation
id
admits the
least
flexibility, whereas the mutation
R
admits the
most
flexibility. The mutation
G
admits more flexibility than
id
, but less than
R
, so we have the ordering
\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sqsubseteq\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sqsubseteq\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.
The meet operation (written
\sqcap
) computes the greatest lower bound between mutations, thereby selecting the more conservative mutation; it enforces that
\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sqcap\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
,
\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sqcap\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and
\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\sqcap\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Because meet is commutative and idempotent, we also have that
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqcap\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqcap\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqcap\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\}
.

3.3.
Tracking Label Sets

To track mutations for each atom in a formula, we rely on
mutation-tracking labels
, written
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathbf{l}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}
where
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}
is the allowable mutation level for the atom labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathbf{l}}
.
To track the evolution of mutations for each label over the course of analyzing a proof, each usage-aware sequent
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{i}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{j}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
in a proof computes sets of mutation-tracking labels
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
(
label sets
for short), defined below.
The
input
set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
(to be preserved) contains mutations that the programmer allows for each label, and the
output
set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
(to be updated) contains mutations that the analysis allows for each label.

\displaystyle\textit{Label Set}\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle::=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\cdot\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathbf{l}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\Sigma

To add or remove from a label set, we define operators
merge
(
\uplus
) and
discard
(
\setminus
).

Merge (
\uplus
).

Fig.
4
defines
\uplus
over label sets
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}
, which has commutative and identity properties (Lines 1 and 2).
When a label
l_{\mathcal{M}}
in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}
does not appear in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}
, then the remainder of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}
merge and
l_{\mathcal{M}}
in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}
is added to the resulting set by a normal set union (Line 3).
Line 4 gives a similar definition for a fused label occurring in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}
where all of its singletons do not occur in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}
.

Otherwise,
\uplus
merges mutations of the same label appearing in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}
, choosing the more conservative mutation level (or meet) between the two occurrences.

Line 5 merges singletons, Line 6 merges a fused label with a singleton, and Line 7 merges fused labels. The resulting fused label in Line 7 may contain duplicate occurrences of singletons, but these are harmless because fused duplicates of the same singleton carry the same semantic meaning as the singleton itself.

\displaystyle\uplus:\mathit{Label\,Set}\times\mathit{Label\,Set}\rightarrow\mathit{Label\,Set}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\cdot\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{1},l_{\mathcal{M}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\right),\color[rgb]{0,0,1}l_{\mathcal{M}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle(\text{if }\color[rgb]{0,0,1}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\notin\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{1},{i_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}{i_{n}}_{\mathcal{M}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma\uplus\Sigma_{2}),{i_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}{i_{n}}_{\mathcal{M}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle(\text{if $\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\notin\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}$})

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{1},l_{\mathcal{M}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{2},l_{\mathcal{N}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\right),\color[rgb]{0,0,1}l_{\mathcal{M}\sqcap\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{1},{i_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}{i_{n}}_{\mathcal{M}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{2},{l}_{\mathcal{N}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{1},{i_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}{i_{n}}_{\mathcal{M}\sqcap\mathcal{N}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle(\text{if $\color[rgb]{0,0,1}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\}$})

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{1},{i_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}{i_{m}}_{\mathcal{M}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma_{2},{{j_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}{j_{n}}}_{\mathcal{N}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\Sigma_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma_{2}\right)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\uplus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{{{i_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}i_{m}{\curlywedgeuparrow}j_{1}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}j_{n}}}_{\mathcal{M}\sqcap\mathcal{N}}\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle(\text{if $\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{j}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\neq\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\emptyset$})

Figure 4.
Definition of merge operator
\uplus

Discard (
\setminus
).

We define the discard operator for recursively removing singleton labels from a label set (Fig.
5
), where we write
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
as shorthand for
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;(\forall\,\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
.
Line 1 defines discarding labels from an empty label set, which produces the empty set.
Line 2 defines discarding a singleton label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l
from a label set that contains a matching singleton label, which is the usual set difference.
Line 3 discards
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{1}
from a fused label, which produces a fused label without
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{1}
.
The commutativity of fused labels extends the definition on Line 3 to allow discarding a singleton from
any
position in a fused label, not just the first.
Line 4 defines discarding a label that does not appear in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
, which is just
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
with remaining
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l^{\prime}}
discarded.
Line 5 is the base case, defining the removal of no labels from
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
to be just
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
.

\displaystyle\setminus:\textit{Label Set}\times\textit{Label Vector}\rightarrow\textit{Label Set}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\cdot\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\setminus\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\cdot

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma,{l}_{\mathcal{M}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{l},\vec{l^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma,{l_{1}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{l}_{2}}}_{\mathcal{M}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{l}_{1},\vec{l^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\Sigma,{\mathbf{l}_{2}}_{\mathcal{M}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l,\vec{l^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\qquad\qquad{(\text{if}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\notin\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\setminus\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cdot\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma

Figure 5.
Definition of discard operator
\setminus

Properties.

In
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{i}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{j}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
, sets
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
are well-formed (Lemma
3.4
), and the labels in input
\Psi
also appear in output
\Sigma
(Property
2
).
A label set is well-formed if each label in it appears only once. Assuming that the input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
does not contain duplicate labels, we show that the output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
also does not contain duplicates, which is preserved due to the semantics of merge and discard.

Lemma 3.4 (
Well-formed sets
).

If
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{i}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{j}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
is valid and the labels in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
are unique, then for all
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
each label in
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}
appears exactly once in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
.

Property
2
enforces that the output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
mentions at least every label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}
in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
but with possibly stricter mutations (
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}
where
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqsubseteq\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}
). In other words, from input to output, the analysis only contributes new information, tracking new labels as they are encountered and enforcing stricter mutations when needed.

Property 2 (
Output subsumes input
).

If
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{i}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{j}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
is valid, then
\forall\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
,
\exists\,\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqsubseteq\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
.

Together, Lemma
3.4
and Property
2
ensure that
UAPC
yields information about every atom in a given formula, and that the computed mutations in the output set respect any constraints given in the input set.

3.4.
Applying Mutations

The output of the analysis is a set mutation-tracking labels. In a separate model improvement step, a programmer may apply to the formula one tracked mutation for each label in the set.
To simulate applying mutations, we define the
mutation instance

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
that chooses a single mutation for each label in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
(Fig.
6
).

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle:\mathit{Stmt}\to\mathit{Stmt},\mathit{Fml}\to\mathit{Fml}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\lnot P)

\displaystyle\triangleq\lnot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P\bowtie Q)

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P)\bowtie{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(Q)

\displaystyle\text{where}~{\bowtie}\in\{\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\leftrightarrow\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\mathcal{Q}\,x\,P)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{Q}\,x\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P)

\displaystyle\text{where}~{\mathcal{Q}}\in\{\forall,\exists\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\mathopen{\mathrel{\langle}\joinrel\mathrel{[}}\alpha\mathclose{\mathrel{]}\joinrel\mathrel{\rangle}}P)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathopen{\mathrel{\langle}\joinrel\mathrel{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\alpha)\mathclose{\mathrel{]}\joinrel\mathrel{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P)

\displaystyle\text{where}~{\mathopen{\mathrel{\langle}\joinrel\mathrel{[}}\cdot\mathclose{\mathrel{]}\joinrel\mathrel{\rangle}}}\in\{\left[\cdot\right],\langle\cdot\rangle\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(?Q)

\displaystyle\triangleq\;?{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(Q)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\alpha\triangleright\beta)

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\alpha)\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\beta)

\displaystyle\text{where}~{\triangleright}\in\{\cup,;\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\alpha^{*})

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\alpha)^{*}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(x^{\prime}=f(x)\;\&\;Q)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}x^{\prime}=f(x)\;\&\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(Q)\;&\quad{\text{if}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}:x^{\prime}=f(x),\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma},\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}\in\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\\
?{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(Q)\;&\quad{\text{if}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}:x^{\prime}=f(x),\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma},\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\alpha_{\text{atom}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}(\alpha_{\text{atom}})\;&\quad{\text{if}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}:\alpha_{\text{atom}},\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}}\\
\alpha_{\text{atom}}\;&\quad{\text{else}}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P_{\text{atom}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}(P_{\text{atom}})\;&\quad{\text{if}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}:P_{\text{atom}},\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}}\\
P_{\text{atom}}\;&\quad{\text{else}}\end{cases}

Figure 6.
Definition of mutation instance
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
maps statements to statements, and formulas to formulas, performing a recursive traversal until it reaches the atoms (Lines 1-7).
The exception is differential equations (Line 8) which we handle in combination with their domain constraints. For differential equations, the mutations
id
and
G
are treated the same. The
R
mutation replaces the entire differential equation and its domain constraint
Q
with
?Q
, because differential equations are statements.

At the atomic level (Lines 9-10),
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
applies a mutation
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}
to an atomic statement (
\alpha_{\text{atom}}
) or atomic formula (
P_{\text{atom}}
) labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}
if
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
and the applied mutation
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}
is bounded by the computed mutation
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{N}
(
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{N}
).
Otherwise, if the atom’s label is not in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
does not apply a mutation to the atom, skipping over it.
This case only triggers when the input provided contains a label irrelevant to the formula being analyzed, because Lemma
3.4
ensures that every label in a formula is analyzed and appears exactly once in the output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
.
The fact that labels appear exactly once in a given output set enforces that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
applies exactly one mutation per label.

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
as shorthand for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P_{1}),\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P_{n})\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(Q_{1}),\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(Q_{m})
where
P_{i}\in\Gamma
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
Q_{j}\in\Delta
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
Statements in Fig.
6
are accompanied by the side condition
{\textit{Var}\,({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\alpha))}\subseteq{\textit{Var}\,(\alpha)}

which enforces that mutations do not introduce new variables (which will be important for defining axioms in Sec.
4.2
).
Generalizations to systems of differential equations follow accordingly.

Fused labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{l_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\dots}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{l_{n}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}
place an additional constraint on mutation.
Because the fused labels represent cross-labeled atoms, the mutation instance can choose any mutation allowed by
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}
for
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{1},\dots,l_{n}

as long as they are the same
; any mutation of the atom labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{1}
must match the mutation of the atom labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{2}
, and so on.
Defn.
3.5
formalizes this property by restricting the mutation operator to apply the same mutation
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}m
to the atoms labeled by singletons
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{1},\dots,l_{n}
occurring in fused label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{l_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\dots}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{l_{n}}
.

Definition 3.5 (Fusing).

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma,{l_{1}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\dots}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{l_{n}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}}}(\Gamma\vdash P)
is valid iff
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{l_{1}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{m}},\dots,{l_{n}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}}(\Gamma\vdash P))
is valid for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqsubseteq\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathcal{M}
.

We write, for example,
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{{i}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{m}},{j}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}}
to denote a
concrete
mutation instance instantiated with concrete mutations
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{m}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{n}}
that obey the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
.
For example, if
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{i_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},{j}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}
, one concrete mutation instance could be
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{i_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},j_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}}
, but not
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{j_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},j_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}}
because multiple mutations for
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j
are instantiated and a mutation for
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i
is not included, violating Lemma
3.4
.

Properties.

While
UAPC
strives to compute the most flexible mutations when possible, Property
3
enforces that
id
is always a safe choice for any label in a formula. Additionally, the same mutations can be applied to a valid formula in any order, all resulting in the same mutated formula (Lemma
3.6
).

Property 3 (
Identity admissibility
).

If
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
is valid, then
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{\vec{\mathbf{i}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
is valid.

Lemma 3.6 (
Composition of mutations
).

If
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
are sets s.t.
\forall\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\notin\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
, then
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\cup\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\equiv\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta))\equiv\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta))
.

3.5.
Takeaways

In this section, we introduced usage-aware sequents.
They extend
dL
sequents with labels and label sets to help track mutations of atoms.
For example, consider the following valid sequent from Fig.
2
:

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8},u_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I(x_{0},v_{0}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{j,k,l}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L(x_{0},v_{0}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{n}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t=0,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{r}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v=v_{0}\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{DA(\dots{\mid}\Psi)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{s}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-g+rv^{2}\geq-g

where

•

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8},u_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I(x_{0},v_{0})\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{5}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{0}\geq 0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{6}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{0}<0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{7}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{0}>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{8}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r_{\textit{cl}}=0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{u_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x>-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}

•

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{j,k,l}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L(x,v)\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{k}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=r_{cl}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{l}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x>100}

•

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{o}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}

•

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\dots{\mid}\Psi)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{8}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{5}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{6}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{7}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{u_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{o}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}
.

While we define
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\dots{\mid}\Psi)
in the next section, we can already see that the output set is a function of the input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
.
The computed output set indicates that atoms labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s
, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{o}}
should remain unchanged (
id
),
r_{cl}=0
(labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{8}
) could be generalized to
r_{cl}\geq 0
(
G
, supposing that
d^{*}
is instantiated with the same constant
0
), and all other atoms could be removed (
R
) while preserving validity.
For example, we could choose a concrete mutation instance
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{DA(\dots{\mid}\Psi)}
instantiated with exactly these mutations such that when applied:

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{DA(\dots{\mid}\Psi)}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I(x_{0},v_{0}),L(x_{0},v_{0}),t=0,v=v_{0}\vdash-g+rv^{2}\geq-g\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\right)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},r=r_{\textit{cl}},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},r_{\textit{cl}}\geq 0\vdash-g+rv^{2}\geq-g\right)

The valid mutated sequent above is logically equivalent to
r=r_{\textit{cl}},r_{\textit{cl}}\geq 0\vdash-g+rv^{2}\geq-g
, which verifies our intuition that
r
is non-negative is the only information needed to prove that
-g+rv^{2}\geq-g
.
The fact that all other atoms in this formula were unused makes us question whether all atoms of
L(x_{0},v_{0})
and the loop invariant
I(x,v)
were necessary, but so far our analysis has only been local. In the next section, we introduce a proof-based mechanism for computing mutations of the original conjecture by computing mutations across proof steps.

4.
Usage-Aware Proof Calculus (
UAPC
)

In this section, we give the proof rules of
UAPC
which define the interaction between usage-aware sequents in a proof.
We annotate the core
dL
proof rules
(
che [n. d.]
)
with labels and label sets, and define how they propagate information in each proof step.
We show a few rules to explain the mechanics of
UAPC
and give the full calculus in Appendix
B
.

4.1.
Inductive Proof Rules

\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\lor$R}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\lor\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\to$R}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\to\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\land$R}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\land$L}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}WL}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma\uplus\{{\vec{\mathbf{i}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\forall$R}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(y),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\forall x\;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(x),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{(y\notin\Gamma,\Delta,\forall x\;p(x))}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\exists$R}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(e),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\exists x\;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(x),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{(\text{arbitrary term $e$})}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}loop}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Theta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{m}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{(\Sigma\uplus\Omega\uplus\Theta)\setminus{\vec{l}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{m}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{*}]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{(\forall J_{a}\in\text{atoms}(J).\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{n_{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}={\phi(J_{a},(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})})}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}cut}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{(\Sigma\uplus\Omega)\setminus{\vec{l}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{(\forall C_{a}\in\text{atoms}(C).\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j_{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}={\phi(C_{a},(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})})}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}GVR}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\text{const}}\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta_{\text{const}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega\uplus\{{\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\left[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\right]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}dI}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{m}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}:=f(x)]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P)^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}=f(x)\&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{m}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}dC}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}=f(x)\&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{m}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}=f(x)\&(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C)]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{m}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}=f(x)\&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{m}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}CER}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{m}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\leftrightarrow\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{(\Omega\uplus\Sigma)\setminus{\vec{l}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{m}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{(\forall Q_{a}\in\text{atoms}(Q).\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j_{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}={\phi(Q_{a},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})})}

Figure 7.
Representative
UAPC
inductive proof rules

Fig.
7
shows a representative selection of
UAPC
inductive proof rules, which propagate the
input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
from conclusion to premise
and the
output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
from premise to conclusion
.

The rules
M:
\lor
R,
M:
\rightarrow
R,
M:
\land
L,
M:
\forall
R, and
M:
\exists
R all preserve the same output sets from premise to conclusion because the labels in the conclusion match the ones in the premise, and no new information is learned in these steps.
The rule
M:
\forall
R eliminates the quantifier
\forall x
by binding a variable
y
to every free instance of
x
in the predicate
p(x)
. From conclusion to premise, the formula
p(x)
transforms into
p(y)
, maintaining the same labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}
. The restriction
y\notin\Gamma,\Delta,\forall x\;p(x)
ensures that the concretizing variable
y
does not clash with other variables. Analogously, the rule
M:
\exists
R eliminates an existential quantifier from conclusion to premise by instantiating
x
with an arbitrary term
e
.

In the rule
M:
\land
R, the first premise has output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
and the second has output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
. The output set of the conclusion is the merge of these two
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\uplus\Omega
because the conclusion mentions labels in both of the premises. The merge in the conclusion (defined in Fig.
4
) ensures that mutations computed in each of the premises agree.

Instead of proving the conclusion directly, the rules
M:
cut and
M:
loop introduce an auxiliary formula which they use to prove intermediary results in their premises. Notably, the rule
M:
loop introduces a loop invariant
J
and shows that it (1) holds under the assumptions
\Gamma
, (2) implies the postcondition, and (3) holds after one loop iteration provided that it held previously.
The output set of the conclusion is obtained by merging the output sets of the three premises
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
, and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Theta
sans the fresh labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}
, which do not occur in the conclusion and hence do not inform the analysis of the original formula.
The fresh labels are introduced by the loop invariant
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J
where
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{n}}
is a combination of pre-existing and new atoms. To compute
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{n}}
, we define a function
\phi
(in analogy to SSA
(
Cytron et al. 1991
)
) in Defn.
4.1
. The function
\phi
decides whether each atom in
J
is new or already appears elsewhere in the deriving sequent (in
M:
loop, the first premise). If an atom is new, it is assigned a fresh label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{f}
from a predetermined set of fresh singleton labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.
If the atom is not new, then it preserves the same label used for other occurrences of that atom in the labeling context (in
M:
loop, the other labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\mathbf{i}}
in the deriving sequent). We use a fused label to cross-label in cases where there are duplicate atoms in the labeling context.
We write
\text{atoms}(J)
to extract the atoms of formula
J
, and
\text{atoms}(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J)
to extract the labeled atoms of
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J
, and similarly for statements.

Definition 4.1 (
\phi
function).
