\displaystyle\phi:\textit{Atom}\times\{\textit{Labeled Atom}\}\times\textit{Label Vector}\rightarrow\textit{Labeled Atom}

\displaystyle{\phi(\text{atom},\text{lbl ctx},\text{fresh labels})}=\begin{cases}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j_{1}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\dots{\curlywedgeuparrow}j_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}&\textit{if }\exists\;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j_{1},\dots,j_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textit{ s.t. }\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{:}\text{atom},\dots,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{:}\text{atom}\in\text{lbl ctx}\\
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}&\textit{ else, for some $\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l_{f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in$ }\text{fresh labels}\end{cases}

For example, in the
M:
loop proof step of Fig.
2
,
\phi(
_
,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{i}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
computes a label for each of the four atoms in
I(x,v)
.
Since
x\geq 0
appears in
A
, we reuse its label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{5}
(
\phi(x\geq 0,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{i}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{5}
). Similarly, the labels of
v<0
and
v>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}
, which also appear in
A
, are
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{6}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{7}
. The atom
x>-1
, however, is new as it does not appear in
A
. So it deserves a fresh label (
\phi(x>-1,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{i}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A,\vec{u})=\color[rgb]{1,0,0}u_{1}
).

The rule
M:
WL proves its conclusion without the assumption
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{i}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P
in the premise. The output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\uplus{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}
indicates that removing
P
is allowed.
Similarly, the rule
M:
GVR has output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\uplus{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}
which indicates that
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{\mathbf{j}}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha
is not needed to prove the conclusion. In
M:
GVR,
\Gamma_{\text{const}}
and
\Delta_{\text{const}}
are
\Gamma
and
\Delta
restricted to formulas whose free variables do not intersect with the bound variables of
\alpha
. We label both
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{\text{const}}
with
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}
because the labels of
\Gamma_{\text{const}}
draw from the same labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}
in the conclusion.

The rules
M:
dI and
M:
dC give two ways of using differential equations in a proof. The differential invariant rule
M:
dI treats the differential equation
x^{\prime}=f(x)
as an assignment (second premise) given that the domain constraint implies the postcondition (first premise). In the second premise, the postcondition
P
(once dependent on
x
) transforms into
P^{\prime}
(now dependent on
x^{\prime}
) and moves the domain constraint (which is an “assumption" about the physics) to the assumptions. The rule
M:
dC is the analog of
M:
cut to differential equations, injecting a cut into the proof to help with proving the conclusion. The first premise proves that the cut
C
holds in all states after running the differential equation.
The second premise assumes
C
in the domain constraint of the different equation to prove the post-condition.

The contextual equivalence rule
M:
CER applies equivalence axioms (discussed next in Sec.
4.2
) to replace formulas with their equivalents in any context
C(\cdot)
. As with
M:
loop,
M:
CER analyzes one proof branch at a time.
The second premise gives an equivalence between
P
and
Q
. We use
\phi
to compute labels for
Q
because some of the atoms in
Q
come from
P
while others are new. The first premise plugs in
Q
for all free occurrences of
P
in
C(\cdot)
.

4.2.
Axioms

We use contextual equivalence to apply axioms in a proof.
We first discuss a selection of representative axioms in Fig.
8
and then show how to use them in a proof.

M:
[?]

\displaystyle\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[?\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\leftrightarrow\left(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\to\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\right)

M:
[{;}]

\displaystyle\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{i}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\leftrightarrow[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha][\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P

M:
\langle\cdot\rangle

\displaystyle\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\neg\left[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\right]\neg\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\leftrightarrow\langle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\rangle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P

M:
V

\displaystyle\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p\rightarrow\left[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\right]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p\qquad\qquad\qquad{(\text{$FV(p)\cap BV(\alpha)=\emptyset$})}

M:
[:=]_{1}

\displaystyle\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x:=e\;]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(x)\leftrightarrow\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(e)\qquad{(x\in FV(p(x)))}

M:
[:=]_{2}

\displaystyle\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}\uplus\Psi}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x:=e\;]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p\leftrightarrow\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p\qquad\qquad{(x\notin FV(p))}

M:
[\cup]

\displaystyle\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{i}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{k}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{l}}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\cup\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{\mathbf{k}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{l}}:}{P}\leftrightarrow\left([\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\right)

Figure 8.
Representative
UAPC
axioms

The axiom
M:
[?] converts between box tests (
[?Q]P
) and implications (
Q\rightarrow P
). The mutation
R
means that
Q
and
P
could be uniformly removed from both sides while preserving the proof.
We use the same labels on the left and right to ensure that mutations are applied uniformly to both sides.
Axioms
M:
[;] and
M:
\langle\cdot\rangle
are similar in their labeling schemes.
M:
[;] gives the sequencing rule for hybrid programs enveloped in box modalities, splitting
[\alpha;\beta]P
into
[\alpha][\beta]P
, and vice versa.
M:
\langle\cdot\rangle
translates between box and diamond modalities by duality.

Axiom
M:
V reasons about hybrid programs
\alpha
that do not bind variables in the postcondition
p
. If
p
holds, then
[\alpha]p
holds given that the bound variables in
\alpha
do not clash with the free variables of
p
, which is preserved even after mutation by definition because the variables of mutated
\alpha
is a subset of the variables already in
\alpha
.
The axioms M:
[:=]_{1}
and M:
[:=]_{2}
handle assignments in a proof by substituting free occurrences of
x
in the postcondition
p
with
e
(M:
[:=]_{1}
), or disregarding it when there are no free occurrences of
x
in
p
to substitute (M:
[:=]_{2}
). The former enforces that
x:=e
remains identical (
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}j_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}
) under mutation because it is used to prove
p
, whereas the latter allows
x:=e
to be removed (
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}j_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}
) because it is not needed to prove
p
. The labels of
p
(
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{k}}
) are the same on both sides, and they can be uniformly removed on both sides while preserving the proof.

Lastly, we explain how to apply axioms in a proof by using the axiom
M:
\left[\cup\right]
as an example. Axiom
M:
\left[\cup\right]
reasons about nondeterministic choice. Since
M:
\left[\cup\right]
is bidirectional, it may be applied in two different ways (forward
\rightarrow
or backward
\leftarrow
). We explore both possibilities in Fig.
9
. When applied in the backward direction (Fig.
9
(B)), the conclusion of the proof containing
[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\cup\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{k}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{k}}}:}{P}
is transformed into the premise containing
[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P
. Alternatively, when applied in the forward direction (Fig.
9
(A)), the conclusion contains
[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P
.
However, the premise has only one occurrence of
P
. To ensure proper label tracking, we assign a fused label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{k}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{l}}}
to preserve both
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{k}}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{l}}
. The label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{k}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{l}}}
means that each atom in
P
is assigned label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathbf{k}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{l}}
for the appropriate
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{k}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{l}
.
In
M:
[\cup]
, the mutation
R
on
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{\mathbf{k}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{l}}}
indicates that we could uniformly remove all occurrences of
P
(labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{k}}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{l}}
) on both sides of the axiom while preserving the proof.

\qquad{(\text{A})}\;\;\frac{{\begin{aligned} &\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{m}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C({\hbox{\pagecolor{pink}$\displaystyle[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\cup\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{\mathbf{k}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{l}}:}{P}$})},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{o}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad&\cdot\;\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\vec{\mathbf{i}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{k}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{l}}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\cup\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{\mathbf{k}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{l}}:}{P}\leftrightarrow\left([\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\right)\end{aligned}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega\uplus\{{\vec{\mathbf{i}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{k}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{l}}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{m}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C({\hbox{\pagecolor{pink}$\displaystyle[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P$}}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{o}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\qquad{(\text{B})}\;\;\frac{{\begin{aligned} &\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{m}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C({\hbox{\pagecolor{pink}$\displaystyle[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P$}}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{o}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad&\cdot\;\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\vec{\mathbf{i}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{k}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{k}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\cup\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{\mathbf{k}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{k}}:}{P}\leftrightarrow\left([\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\right)\end{aligned}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{n}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega\uplus\{{\vec{\mathbf{i}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{k}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{k}}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{m}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C({\hbox{\pagecolor{pink}$\displaystyle[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha\cup\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\beta]{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overrightarrow{\mathbf{k}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\mathbf{k}}:}{P}$}}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{o}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}

Figure 9.
Using axiom
M:
[\cup]
with contextual equivalence rule
M:
CER in two different styles

4.3.
Leaf Rules

To close a proof, we apply leaf rules (for example, the
M:
QE step in Fig.
2
).
Leaf rules do not have any premises and provide the base cases of the proof; when given a concrete input set, each leaf rule returns a concrete output set that is a function of the input set and labels present in that proof step.
We give all of the
UAPC
leaf rules in Fig.
10
.

\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}id}}\;\;\frac{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{l}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}QE}}\;\;\frac{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\top$R}}\;\;\frac{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{\mathbf{i}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{i}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{true},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathbb{R}$}}\;\;\frac{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\bot$L}}\;\;\frac{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{i}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{false},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{\mathbf{i}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}auto}}\;\;\frac{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}

Figure 10.
UAPC
leaf rules

The rule
M:
id closes a proof if one of the assumptions is syntactically equal to a succedent
P
.
Given an input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
,
M:
id computes the output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{j}}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\vec{\mathbf{l}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}\uplus\Psi
.
The fused label
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{j}}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}
indicates that
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}
are allowed to (uniformly) take on at most the mutation
R
because the rule only requires that the antecedent
P
matches the succedent
P
, which would hold even if both instances were mutated to
\mathit{true}
.

\Gamma
and
\Delta
are each mutable under
R
as they could be removed while preserving the proof.

The rules
M:
\mathbb{R}
and
M:
auto take an input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
and produce an output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)
, defined according to Defn.
4.2
. When applied, the mutations in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)
yield a valid sequent.
Additionally, each label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}
appears in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)
exactly once, which is necessary to preserve the property that
UAPC
analyzes every atom in a formula.
We leave the concrete instantiation of
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)
to future implementations of
UAPC
.

Definition 4.2 (
Dynamic Analysis
).

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)
is a set of mutation-tracking labels such that:

•

If
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{{DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
is valid, then
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
is valid for any
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)}
.

•

Each label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}
appears exactly once in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\vec{\mathbf{i}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}{\mid}\Psi)
.

4.4.
Takeaways

The
UAPC
proof rules propagate label sets through a proof to learn usage information for each label.
Given a concrete input set, leaf rules return a concrete output set with information about every label in that step.
Rules that have multiple premises
merge
their output sets into the conclusion output set. Rules that use
\phi
discard fresh labels from their output sets because they are not mentioned in the conclusion, and hence do not give usage information about the starting formula.
If these fresh labels were not discarded, however, they would yield usage diagnostics of cuts in a proof.
Appendix
D.7
formulates such a calculus.

UAPC
is flexible in allowing different ways of instantiating the constraint tracking.
The core calculus explores proof branches in parallel and merges their usage information in each step.
In Appendix
D
, we formulate
UAPC
+
as the sequential counterpart of
UAPC
which threads label sets through branches, thereby guiding the analysis of each proof branch with the output of the prior.
Due to its sequential analysis, proof rules in
UAPC
+
have the advantage of being defined without merge, which could reduce computational overhead in future implementations of
UAPC
.

5.
Key Theorems

We prove soundness and completeness of
UAPC
with respect to
dL
. We show representative proof cases for both theorems and motivate key lemmas when needed in the proofs.
Full proofs of all results are in Appendix
C
.

5.1.
UAPC
is correct.

To show the correctness of mutations computed by
UAPC
, we prove that it is
sound
with respect to
dL
. Soundness means that if a formula has a proof in
UAPC
, then all of its computed mutations also have a proof in the
dL
sequent calculus (Theorem
5.1
).

Theorem 5.1 (
Soundness
).

If
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
is valid, then
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
is valid for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
.

The proof is by structural induction on the proof of
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
.
We highlight a couple of representative cases here, M:id (representing the base cases) and M:cut (representing the inductive cases). We use boxes to distinguish key lemmas from the rest of the proof.

Case M:id. :

Suppose the last rule in the proof tree is M:id:

\quad{\text{M:id}}\;\;\frac{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{l}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma}
represent
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{l}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}
.

Our goal is to show that
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P,\Gamma\vdash P,\Delta)
is valid for any
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Psi}
.

We first show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P,\Gamma\vdash P,\Delta)
is valid for any
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
. Then, we extend the result to
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Psi}
.

Our strategy is to
eliminate what we don’t need
, which are
\Gamma
and
\Delta
for
M:
id.
Fix an arbitrary
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
. Observe that if we can show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P\vdash P)
is valid, then by the
dL
rule
W
LR, we can show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P,\Gamma\vdash P,\Delta)
is valid:

\quad{\text{{W}LR}}\;\;\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P\vdash P)}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P,\Gamma\vdash P,\Delta)}

We need to show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P\vdash P)
is valid.

By Defn.
3.5
and the definition of
\mu
in Fig.
6
, the mutation
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}m
selected for both labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{i}}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{j}}
must satisfy
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}m\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sqsubseteq\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. We have one case for each possible
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}m
(three in total). We elide mutations for
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{k}}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{l}}
from
\mu
to focus the argument because
\Gamma
and
\Delta
do not appear in
P\vdash P
.

Subcase 1:
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}m}=\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.
By definition of
\mu
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P\vdash P)\equiv\left(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{\vec{\mathbf{i}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{\vec{\mathbf{i}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P)\right)\equiv\left(P\vdash P\right)

Subcase 2:
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}m}=\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.
By definition of
\mu
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P\vdash P)\equiv\left(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{\vec{\mathbf{i}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P)\vdash\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{\vec{\mathbf{i}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}G\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P)\right)\equiv\left(\tilde{P}\vdash\tilde{P}\right)

Subcase 3:
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}m}=\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.
By definition of
\mu
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P\vdash P)\equiv\left(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{\vec{\mathbf{i}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P)\vdash\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\{\vec{\mathbf{i}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P)\right)\equiv\left(true\vdash true\right)

By
dL
rule id, each sequent is valid.

We have just shown that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(P\vdash P)
is valid.

We can now apply
W
LR to learn that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\Gamma,P\vdash P,\Delta)
is valid.

Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
was arbitrary, the result holds for
any

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P,\Gamma\vdash P,\Delta)
.

To finish the proof, we need to show that
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma,P\vdash P,\Delta)
is valid for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Psi}
(Lemma
5.2
).
The key intuition is that
introducing more mutational constraints limits the overall pool of mutations for a given atom
due to the semantics of merge
\uplus
. In short, the set of labels may grow, but the mutations can only become more conservative.

Lemma 5.2 (
Monotonicity
).

If
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
is valid for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma_{1}}
, then
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma_{1}\uplus\Sigma_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
is valid for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma_{1}\uplus\Sigma_{2}}
.

By Lemma
5.2
, we have:

\mathbf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{{\{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{l}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P,\Gamma\vdash P,\Delta)\textbf{ is valid for all }\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{{\{\overrightarrow{{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{l}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}}\uplus\Psi}}
.

Case M:cut. :

Suppose the last rule in the proof tree is M:cut:

\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}cut}^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{(\Sigma\uplus\Omega)\setminus{\vec{l}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}\quad{(\forall C_{a}\in\text{atoms}(C).\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j_{a}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}={\phi(C_{a},(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})})}

We want to show that
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{(\Sigma\uplus\Omega)\setminus{\vec{l}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
is valid.

Because the last rule is M:cut, we know that there must exist
smaller
proofs of

(1):

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta

(2):

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C

By application of the IH to (1) and (2), we have:

(3):

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}(\Gamma,C\vdash\Delta)
is valid for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma}}
.

(4):

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta,C)
is valid for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}
.

By Lemma
5.2
applied to (3):

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma,C\vdash\Delta)\;\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{ is valid for \emph{all} $\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}$.}\;\;\;(5)

Fix an arbitrary
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}
. By Lemma
5.2
applied to (4):

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta,C)\;\text{ is valid for \emph{the same fixed} $\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}$}.\;\;\;(6)

By the
dL
proof rule cut applied to (5) and (6):

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\;\text{ is valid for \emph{fixed} $\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}$}.\;\;\;(7)

Because
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}
was arbitrary, we know that (7) holds
for all

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}
.

To finish the proof, we introduce Corollary
5.3
which shows that
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{(\Sigma\uplus\Omega)\setminus\vec{l}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\;\text{ is valid}
for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{(\Sigma\uplus\Omega)\setminus\vec{l}}
. Discarding labels
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}
is safe because they are fresh, meaning that they do not appear in
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
. Hence, their mutations could not affect
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.

Corollary 5.3 (
Fresh monotonicity
).

If
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma_{1}}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
is valid for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma_{1}}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}
is fresh, then
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma_{1}\setminus{\vec{l}}}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)
is valid for all
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Sigma_{1}\setminus{\vec{l}}}
.

Because
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{l}
is fresh, the desired result follows directly by Corollary
5.3
:

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{(\Sigma\uplus\Omega)\setminus{\vec{l}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\;\text{ is valid for {all} $\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{(\Sigma\uplus\Omega)\setminus{\vec{l}}}$}.

5.2.
UAPC
can analyze any valid
dL
formula.

To show that
UAPC
can analyze any valid
dL
formula, we prove that it is
complete
with respect to
dL
. Completeness means that if a formula has a proof in the
dL
sequent calculus, then for all input sets
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
there exists an output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
such that the formula has a proof in
UAPC
(Theorem
5.4
).

Theorem 5.4 (
Completeness
).

If
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is valid, then
\forall\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi

\exists\,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
s.t.
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta
is valid.

The proof is by structural induction on the dL proof of
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
. We highlight a few interesting cases,
\land
R, [:=], and id.

Case
\land
R. :

Suppose the last rule in the proof tree is
\land
R:

\quad{\text{$\land$R}}\;\;\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{P}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{Q}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{P}\land{Q}}

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
be an arbitrary input set.
By
\land
R, there exist
smaller
proofs of

(1):

\Gamma\vdash P

(2):

\Gamma\vdash Q

By the IH applied to (1) and (2), there exist output sets
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
such that

(3):

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P

(4):

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q

By application of M:
\land
R to (3) and (4), we obtain the desired result with output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma\uplus\Omega
:

\quad{\text{M:$\land$R}}\;\;\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\quad\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\Sigma\uplus\Omega}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\\

Case [:=]. :

Suppose the last rule in the proof tree is
[:=]
:

\quad{[:=]}\;\;\cdot\vdash[{x:=e}]{p(x)}\leftrightarrow{p(e)}

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
be an arbitrary input set. The proof proceeds in two subcases:

(1):

where
x
appears in the postcondition (i.e.
x\in FV(p(x))
) and

(2):

where
x
does not appear in the postcondition (i.e.
x\notin FV(p)
).

Subcase
x\in FV(p(x))

If
x\in FV(p(x))
, then we obtain the desired result by
M:
[:=]_{1}
with output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{\mathbf{j_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}},\vec{\mathbf{k}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi
:

\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$[:=]_{1}$}}\;\;\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{k}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x:=e]\;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(x)\leftrightarrow\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p(e)\;\;\quad{(x\in FV(p(x)))}

Subcase
x\notin FV(p)

If
x\notin FV(p)
, then we obtain the desired result by
M:
[:=]_{2}
with output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{k}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi
:

\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$[:=]_{2}$}}\;\;\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{{\{{\mathbf{j}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\vec{\mathbf{k}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,[\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x:=e]\;\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p\leftrightarrow\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p\;\;\quad{(x\notin FV(p))}

Case id.:

Suppose the last rule in the proof tree is id:

\quad{\text{id}}\;\;\frac{}{{P},{\Gamma}\vdash{P},{\Delta}}

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
be an arbitrary input set. Therefore, the desired result follows by the rule
M:
id with output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{{\vec{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{l}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi
:

\quad{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}M:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}id}}\;\;\frac{}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{i}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{k}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\vdash\mkern-5.0mu{}^{\Psi}_{\{{\vec{\mathbf{i}}{\curlywedgeuparrow}\vec{\mathbf{j}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{k}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\vec{\mathbf{l}}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\}\uplus\Psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{j}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{\mathbf{{l}}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}

All other cases follow a similar proof strategy; identify the last rule in the proof tree and use the assumed valid dL formula and an arbitrary input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
to obtain the desired result.
In each case, we observe that for every dL proof rule, there exists a matching
UAPC
proof rule.

5.3.
Takeaways

UAPC
is both sound and complete, meaning that mutations computed by
UAPC
can be applied to a valid
dL
formula while preserving its proof, and that
UAPC
is general enough to analyze
any
valid
dL
formula.
Our modular proofs of both results allow for straightforward extension to new and derived proof rules.
The modular structure of our base cases (as seen in the
M:
id soundness case) allow for straightforward extension to new mutations.

6.
Parachute Proof (
UAPC
style)

In this section, we revisit the parachute proof from Sec.
2
, this time analyzing with
UAPC
.
The analysis begins at the root of the proof tree shown in the bottom fragment. We suppose the programmer is pretty confident in their physical dynamics, so they provide the initial constraints
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{o}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}
to enforce that the differential equations (labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{o}
) should remain unchanged. The objective is to solve for the output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
in the conclusion, which
UAPC
accomplishes by analyzing the input set and synthesizing an output set in each proof step.

6.1.
The Analysis

The input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
propagates up through each proof step
until it reaches the leaf rules which use it to compute a concrete output set. For example, the
M:
QE step of branch (3h) concludes that
r_{cl}=0
,
r=r_{cl}
, and
-g+rv^{2}\geq-g
should remain unchanged under mutation (
id
).

Now, the output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\dots{\mid}\Psi)
propagates down the proof tree.

In the
M:
[:=]_{2}
step,
UAPC
determines that the assignment
x^{\prime}:=v
is not used in this step and can be safely removed (
R
).
The next two steps (both
M:
[:=]_{2}
) detect that assignments
v^{\prime}:=-g+rv^{2}
and
t^{\prime}:=1
are used and should remain unchanged (
id
).

The output set continues to propagate down to (3a), where the outputs of (3g) and (3h) are merged by
M:
dI, and then again with (3f) by
M:
dC which also discards the fresh label
s
.
The
M:
\land
R step merges the output sets of its premises forming the output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Theta
in the conclusion.
This output set is preserved for the remainder of (3a).

In the bottom snippet, the
M:
loop step merges the output sets of (1), (2), and (3) and discards fresh label
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}u_{1}
, producing the output set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
.
The
M:
\rightarrow
R step preserves this output.
As this rule is the root of the proof tree, the analysis concludes and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
is the final result:

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega=

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{5}{\curlywedgeuparrow}p_{2}}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{6}{\curlywedgeuparrow}p_{3}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{1}\}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{2}\}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{o_{3}\}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{7}\}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}m}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{p_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{1}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{2}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{3}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{4}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}R\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\{i_{8}\}}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{q}_{\text{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}id\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}

6.2.
Model Improvement

From
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega
, the programmer learns that the atoms labelled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}k
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}o_{2}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}o_{3}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{7}
,
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{8}
, and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\vec{q}
should all remain unchanged, with the additional constraint that
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{5}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}p_{2}
, and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i_{6}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}p_{3}
take on the same mutation (which degenerates to just remaining unchanged because
id
is the only mutation allowed).
The atom labeled by
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}o_{1}
(which corresponds to the differential equation
x^{\prime}=v
) must also remain unchanged. Even though
UAPC
determined that
x^{\prime}=v
was not needed to close the proof, the input constraints enforced that it must remain unchanged, which is respected in the output set.
All other atoms can take on any mutation.
In particular, we notice that
x>100
(
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l
) and
v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}
(
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j
), as expected, were unused in the proof, and
UAPC
verifies that these can be safely removed from the formula while preserving its validity.

If the programmer were to apply all these mutations at their extremes, they would learn that the following generalized formula is also valid:

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{A}{\to}\left[\left(\left(?({v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}}\land{r=r_{cl}}\land{x>100})\cup\;r:=r_{op}\right);t:=0;\text{ODE}\&{Q}\right)^{*}\right]P\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\right)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\to}\left[\left(\left(?(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\land{r=r_{cl}}\land\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\cup\;?\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right);t:=0;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{ODE}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{*}\right]\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})

where

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\equiv\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\land\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\land\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\land\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\textbf{true}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\land{v_{max}<-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}}\land{x\geq 0}\land{-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}<v<0}\land{r=r_{cl}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{ODE}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\equiv x^{\prime}=v,v^{\prime}=-g+rv^{2},t^{\prime}=1

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\equiv{t\leq T}\land{x\geq 0}\land{v<0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mu_{\Omega}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\equiv{x=0}\to v\geq v_{max}

Figure 11.
Model of parachute dynamics,
mutated
.

The non-
id
mutated atoms are highlighted in violet bold. By just looking at the number of
true
’s, we see that there are many unused (and unnecessary) constraints in this formula.

6.3.
Takeaways

UAPC
makes studying large proofs of complex
dL
formulas like this one straightforward.
At first glance, it is not immediately obvious that
v-gT>-\sqrt{g/r_{op}}
could be removed from the original formula without affecting validity, yet through systematic analysis,
UAPC
is able to detect this. Additionally, the input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi
could have instead been instantiated with a non-empty set of user-imposed mutations (for example, atom
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}l
should stay
id
) and
UAPC
would account for these constraints too.

The proof-based technique allows us to identify subtle bugs. In this example, they are
formulas
that occur in a test statement and eventually propagate into the assumptions of the proof tree.

7.
Related Work

Classifying modeling errors in CPSs.

(
Fulton and Platzer 2019
)
describes using mutations to synthesize candidate models to be validated against the real-world-environment at runtime.

(
Selvaraj et al. 2022
)
stratifies modeling errors in
dL
formulas into two categories: vacuity and over-specification. Our work
diagnoses
both kinds.

Finding modeling errors in CPSs.

ModelPlex
(
Mitsch and Platzer 2016
)
identifies modeling errors in
dL
formulas at runtime by determining whether or not a model conforms to the real-world (or simulated) environment. Here, we identify modeling errors
prior to runtime
.
CESAR
(
Kabra et al. 2024
)
synthesizes missing hybrid program components according to a specification. Our work instead
debugs an existing program by looking at its proof
to determine possible mutations, thereby enabling proof diagnostics.

Static vacuity detection for temporal logical formulas finds program traces that vacuously satisfy the specification
(
Dokhanchi et al. 2017
)
and checks whether all subformulas of a specification affect its truth
(
Kupferman and Vardi 2003
)
. In
UAPC
, the mutation
R
provides vacuity detection while the mutation
G
identifies over-constrained program components,
diagnosing a more subtle class of bugs not covered by vacuity detection
.

Other work uses adaptive test case generation
(
Bartocci et al. 2022
)
and property-based mutation
(
Bartocci et al. 2023
)
testing to find modeling errors in signal temporal logic
(
Maler and Ničković 2013
)
formulas.

(
Bartocci et al. 2023
)
allows only one mutation to be applied and tested at a time, whereas
UAPC
allows
multiple mutations to be applied at the same time
and uses fusing to reason about potential dependencies between them.
Both approaches
(
Bartocci et al. 2022
;
Bartocci et al. 2023
)
operate directly on the formula whereas our approach operates on the formula’s proof tree which we use to glean
information about both the formula as well as potential proof diagnostics
. To our knowledge, none of these approaches are verified to be
sound or complete
.

Recent work applies weakest precondition and strongest postcondition techniques to detect over-specification in pre- and post-conditions of
dL
formulas
(
Chong et al. 2023
)
. However, these techniques
do not provide the granularity of bug detection that we are pursuing in this work.

UAPC
is capable of diagnosing over-specification of atoms arising all over
dL
formula, not just in the pre- and post-conditions.

Refinement for
dL
.

Prior work uses similar proof-based techniques for finding refactorings of
dL
programs
(
Mitsch et al. 2014
)
. Like
UAPC
, their work aims to find ways
dL
formula could be updated while reusing or transforming the proof.
However, each refactoring has to be justified semantically on its own, whereas
UAPC
embeds such refactorings (here, mutations) into its proof rules which are used to construct a proof that automatically justifies a set of possible refactorings.

Differential refinement logic has been used to simplify
dL
proofs and develop proofs of complex
dL
formulas
(
Loos and Platzer 2016
;
Prebet and Platzer 2024
)
. They relate concrete
dL
formulas to abstract ones which can be proved more easily.

UAPC
is a refinement of the
dL
proof calculus, providing usage information that would not be so easily attained by working in
dL
alone.

UAPC
operates on a given
dL
proof, generating mutations such that when applied, generate a similar proof to the original. Our extension
\texttt{UAPC}_{\texttt{diag}}
diagnoses over-specification and vacuity of cuts in
dL
proofs.

Linear logic.

Linear logic
(
Cervesato et al. 1996
;
Chirimar et al. 1996
)
characterizes the usage of atoms in formula
quantitatively
whereas
UAPC
does so
qualitatively
.
The purposes of linear logic and
UAPC
are orthogonal. Linear logic ensures that resources in a proof are used exactly once whereas
UAPC
’s qualitative analysis of atom usage allows it to detect bugs in formulas, which linear logic was not designed for.
Atom usage in a
dL
proof is non-linear in the sense of linear logic meaning that the same atom may be used in multiple places in a proof. To ensure the soundness of our mutation scheme, it is especially important to reason about the usage of
all
occurrences of a given atom in a proof.
Resource semantics
(
Reed 2009
)
labels formulas and uses a proof calculus to track the
quantitative
usage of atoms in a proof.
Proof normalization and cut elimination operate on proofs
(
Cockett 2001
;
Pfenning 2009
)
while
UAPC
operates on the conjecture itself, producing a mutated proof in the process. In Appendix
D.7
, we formulate an extension of
UAPC
that yields usage information for cuts in a proof.

7.1.
Takeaways

Subsuming vacuity detection
(
Dokhanchi et al. 2017
;
Kupferman and Vardi 2003
;
O’Hearn 2019
)
,
UAPC
identifies over-constrained program components in addition to vacuous ones. Other approaches apply weakest precondition and strongest postcondition techniques
(
Chong et al. 2023
)
, yet they are limited to detecting bugs in a formula’s precondition and postcondition, whereas
UAPC
analyzes every atom in a formula.
To our knowledge, testing-based approaches
(
Bartocci et al. 2022
;
Bartocci et al. 2023
;
Maler and Ničković 2013
)
are not proved to be correct or complete whereas
UAPC
is. Unlike single fault analyses
(
Bartocci et al. 2023
)
,
UAPC
produces mutations for every atom in a formula making it capable of detecting multiple interconnected bugs.

UAPC
also provides proof diagnostics which arises naturally from its proof-based approach that could not be achieved by testing-based techniques.

8.
Conclusion and Future Work

In this work, we developed
UAPC
, a proof-based technique for diagnosing vacuity and over-specification bugs in
dL
formulas.
The resulting system is capable of detecting all possible classes of bugs in
dL
formulas
(
Selvaraj et al. 2022
)
and helps programmers develop more generalized hybrid systems models with stronger safety guarantees.
Our proof-based technique yields several benefits. First, it is capable of detecting bugs in any valid
dL
formula, which is verified by our completeness property. The proof-based technique also lends itself to systematic analysis of every atom in a
dL
formula, and determines combinations of mutations on these atoms.
UAPC
’s soundness property guarantees that any combination of computed mutations preserve the validity of a
dL
formula. We also formulated sound and complete extensions of
UAPC
to proof diagnostics, which could be used to find optimal
dL
proofs, and sequential analysis, allowing future implementations to avoid merging sets.

Future work

The techniques we introduce through
UAPC
are useful for studying model improvement and proof-preserving modifications. In this work, we used the mutations
id
,
G
, and
R
as an example. However, extending to more fine-grained mutations is supported by our meet-semilattice formulation, which would involve defining the new mutations as functions and inserting them between
id
and
R
in the partial order.
For example, we leave generalizing mutations for atomic differential equations to future work, which in this work we took to be the same as the identity mutation.

We leave implementations of
UAPC
,
UAPC
+
, and
\texttt{UAPC}_{\texttt{diag}}
to future work, yet we anticipate them to be straightforward with the proposed formulation. Like all other high-level reasoning and tracking relation among proof steps, our calculi will be implemented outside of the KeYmaera X core
(
Platzer 2015
)
. The soundness and completeness properties proved in this work ensure that the implementations produce mutations and proof steps
guaranteed to succeed in the core
.

UAPC
is also capable of reducing complexity in its implementations.
A naive implementation of
M:
\mathbb{R}
that combinatorially mutates each atom in a formula is infeasible because it involves exponential search on top of a doubly-exponential decision procedure
(
Davenport and Heintz 1988
;
Grebing and Ulbrich 2020
)
which has been a pain point in related techniques
(
Bartocci et al. 2023
)
.
In
UAPC
, the input set
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
in
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}DA(\vec{\mathbf{l}}{\mid}\Psi)
is an initial set of constraints that guides the analysis so that it only needs to test constraints as strict or stricter than the ones provided.

Appendix A
Parachute example

Model.

\vdash\mathbf{A}\to\left[\left(\left(?(\mathbf{v-gT>-\sqrt{g/{r_{op}}}\land r=r_{cl}\land x>100});\cup\;r:=r_{op};\right)t:=0;\text{ODE}\&\mathbf{Q}\right)^{*}\right]P

Parameters

\displaystyle~g,r_{op},m,T,x,v,r,t

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{i}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A

\displaystyle\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g>0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{2}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r_{op}>r_{cl}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{3}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T>0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{4}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{\textit{max}}<-\sqrt{g/{r_{op}}}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{5}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0

\displaystyle\;\;\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{6}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0>v>-\sqrt{g/{r_{op}}}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{7}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=r_{cl}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{8}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r_{cl}=0

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{j,k,l}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L(x,v)

\displaystyle\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{j}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v-gT>-\sqrt{g/{r_{op}}}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{k}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=r_{cl}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{l}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x>100

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{q}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P

\displaystyle\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{q_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=0\to\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{q_{2}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v\geq v_{\textit{max}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{o}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{ODE}

\displaystyle\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{o_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\prime}=v,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{o_{2}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v^{\prime}=-g+rv^{2},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{o_{3}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t^{\prime}=1

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\vec{p}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q

\displaystyle\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{p_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t\leq T,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{p_{2}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{p_{3}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v<0

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8},u_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I(x,v)

\displaystyle\triangleq\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{5}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\geq 0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{6}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v<0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{7}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v>-\sqrt{g/{r_{op}}}\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{i_{8}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r_{cl}=0\land\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{u_{1}}{:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x>-1}

Proof.

The proof tree, using the rules from Section
4
, is presented in boxes: a zig-zag border at the top indicates that the proof continues in another snippet at the annotated subgoals, while a zig-zag border at the bottom indicates that the snippet continues from an open subgoal of another snippet.
The input and output label sets for this example are the following:

\displaystyle\Psi=

in formula
\phi
